4
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
Summary
External environment
In 2016, the world economy was rich in events, which established
new macroeconomic trends at the international level.
The
macroeconomic policies in the advanced economies, concentrated
on consumption stimulation, had a beneﬁcial effect over these
economies and most of emerging and developing economies,
which recovered following the external demand growth and high
international prices of raw materials. An important event in oil
market was the signing by OPEC countries of the agreement on oil
volume production decrease on 30 November 2016.
This
agreement was the main factor of oil prices recovery up to the
value of USD 50 per barrel at the end of 2016, after which, the
price per barrel reached in January 2016 the lowest level since
2004.
The signiﬁcant volatility of foreign exchange markets
represented another important aspect in the world economy. The
USD appreciation trend was driven by the Federal Reserve System
policy to interconnect the interest rates range to the positive
trends of the U.S. economy development. Also, Pound sterling
depreciated signiﬁcantly due to the results of the United Kingdom
European Union membership referendum and further forecasts of
Brexit consequences.
Economic developments
In 2016, the economy of the Republic of Moldova recorded an
increase of 4.1 percent after the slight negative dynamics of
2015. In the ﬁrst half of 2016, GDP recorded an annual modest
dynamic after the drought of 2015 and due to the non-favourable
regional economic climate conditions, an economic uncertainty
and internal policy, which generated an insigniﬁcant domestic
demand and the investment decrease.
The economic growth
was more pronounced in the second half of the year, due mainly
to the favourable meteorological conditions during 2016 that
determined a high performance in agricultural sector. Thus, the
negative dynamic in 2015 was caused by the drought, in 2016 the
gross value added recorded positive developments in agriculture
and in the connected sectors such as: manufacturing industry,
trade and transports. At the same time, the constructions sector
recorded a negative dynamic. In 2016, the positive evolution of
agricultural sector favoured the increase in exports and household
consumption compared to 2015.
Also, the ﬁnal consumption
of public administration and the gross ﬁxed capital recorded a
negative dynamic compared to 2015. The evolution of the change
in inventories subcomponent generated a negative impact on GDP
dynamics in 2016. In 2016, the economic activity dynamics was
signiﬁcantly offset by the increase of imports compared to 2015.


Summary
5
Inﬂation
During 2016, the annual inﬂation rate recorded a pronounced
downward trajectory, decreasing from 13.6 percent in December
2015 to 2.4 percent in December 2016. In August, this fell within
the range of 5.0 percent ±1.5 percentage points stipulated in the
Medium-term monetary policy strategy, accounting for 3.6 percent.
Thereafter, the annual inﬂation rate continued its downward trend,
being below the lower limit of the target range mentioned above.
The average annual inﬂation rate in 2016 was 6.4 percent and was
signiﬁcantly lower than 2015. The inﬂation downward evolution
was due mainly to the modest domestic demand, higher level
of base period in 2015 and to a rich harvest that favoured the
decrease in food prices. At the same time, the downward trajectory
of inﬂation was also determined by the diminution of pressures
from regulated prices, as a result of the downward adjustment
of gas and electricity tariffs in the ﬁrst half of the year and
following the gradual dissipation of the impact of the electricity
tariff increase in summer of 2015.
The evolution of national
currency against the U.S. dollar and the upward trajectory of
food and oil prices on international market offset partially the
disinﬂationary inﬂuence of the aforementioned factors.
Monetary policy
During 2016, there were held twelve meetings of the Executive
Board of the National Bank on Moldova on monetary policy
decisions. Following the assessment of the balance of internal and
external risks, which could be faced by the Moldovan economy
and inﬂation forecasts on medium term, there were adopted ﬁve
decisions to maintain and seven decisions to decrease the base
rate applied to main monetary policy operations from the level of
19.5 percent to 9.0 percent annually.
The required reserves ratio from ﬁnancial means attracted in MDL
and non-convertible currency was maintained at the level of 35.0
percent of the base during 2016. At the same time, the minimum
required reserve ratio attracted in freely convertible currency were
maintained at the level of 14.0 percent during 2016.
The monetary indicators recorded an upward trend in 2016. Thus,
the dynamic of monetary aggregates was positive, the values
registered in annual terms were 18.3 percent for M2 (by 27.4
percentage points more than in 2015) and 10.2 percent for M3 (by
13.2 percentage points lower than in 2015).
During reporting period, the average annual interest rate on
new loans in national currency increased by 0.14 percentage
points, while the average annual interest rate on new loans in
foreign currency decreased by 1.11 percentage points compared
to 2015, recording values of 14.20 percent in national currency
and 5.90 percent in foreign currency. The average interest rate
on deposits in MDL accounted for 10.71 percent, decreasing by
1.39 percentage points compared to 2015, while the interest rate
on placements in foreign currency constituted on average 2.12
percent, decreasing by 0.44 percentage points compared to 2015.


6
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
Banking system
As at 31 December 2016, the banking sector of the Republic
of Moldova included 11 banks licensed by the National Bank of
Moldova, of which 4 are branches of foreign banks and ﬁnancial
groups.
However,
two
banks
(B.C.
„VICTORIABANK”
S.A.
and
BC
„MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK” S.A.) were under intensive supervision.
Intensive supervision (substituted the special supervision, taking
into account the amendments to legislation) is maintained due to
the detection of issues related to non-transparent shareholders’
structure, banks engaged in high risk lending operations and the
unsolving of these issues.
As from 20 October 2016, BC „Moldindconbank” S.A is supervised
within the early intervention regime, as a result of the fact that
a group of persons acquired and held a qualifying holdings in the
bank’s share capital, in the amount of 63.89 percent, without prior
written permission of the NBM, thereby infringing the provisions
of the Law on ﬁnancial institutions. It should be mentioned that
after the application of the early intervention regime, the liquidity
situation of BC „Moldindconbank” S.A. remained stable, without
signiﬁcant withdrawals of deposits as anticipated.
The total number of licensed banks subdivisions was 804, of which
288 branches and 516 agencies. As of 31 December 2016, the
total number of staff employed in the banking sector amounted to
7 868 employees.
Tier I capital of the banking system reached the level of MDL 9
437.8 million, increasing by MDL 429.6 million compared to the
end of 2015 (4.8 percent).
The average of risk-weighted capital adequacy indicator of the
sector recorded the value of 30.1 percent, increasing by 3.8
percentage points compared to the end of 2015.
As of 31
December 2016, all banks have observed the risk-weighted
capital adequacy indicator (the limit for each bank ≥16 percent).
The total assets of the sector were MDL 72 951.4 million, increasing
by MDL 4 161.2 million (6.0 percent) compared with the end of
2015.
Gross loans portfolio was MDL 34 761.3 million, decreasing by MDL
3 425.3 million (9.0 percent) during 2016. The decrease of gross
loans portfolio was determined mainly by the lack of loan demand
and a more cautious approach applied by banks against potential
debtors.
The
non-performing
loans
(substandard,
doubtful,
and
compromised) increased in absolute value by MDL 1 871.3 million
(49.3 percent), amounting to MDL 5 669.9 million, while the share
of non-performing loans in total loans increased by 6.4 percentage
points compared to the end of 2015, accounting for 16.3 percent
as of 31 December 2016. The share of net non-performing loans


Summary
7
in total regulatory capital increased by 4.5 percentage points,
amounting to 19.5 percent as at 31 December 2016.
As at 31 December 2016, the proﬁt of the banking sector
accounted for MDL 1 466.7 million and all banks recorded proﬁt.
The
proﬁt
increased
by
MDL
322.5
million
(28.2
percent)
compared to the end of 2015.
In 2016, the rentability of assets and capital of licensed banks
recorded the value of 2.0 percent and 11.9 percent, respectively,
increasing by 0.3 percentage points and 1.8 percentage points,
respectively, compared to the end of 2015.
Net interest margin was 5.5 percent as at 31 December 2016,
remaining at the level of 2015.


8
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
Chapter 1
External environment
The world economy in 2016 was characterised by the change in
trajectory of the main macroeconomic indicators of international
importance.
The USA economy stability and tightening of the
monetary policy by the Federal Reserve System determined the
gradual appreciation of U.S. dollar during 2016. The appreciation
phenomenon of the USD was also ampliﬁed by the monetary
policy antagonism, European Central Bank and Bank of Japan
continued the quantitative stimulus programs. At the same time,
Pound sterling depreciated signiﬁcantly due to the results of the
Referendum on the United Kingdom member-status within the
European Union and further forecasts of Brexit consequences.
The increase in global demand and the decrease in raw materials
stocks favoured the increase in raw materials prices,
after
approximately two consecutive years of decline.
For example,
OPEC and other productive oil countries negotiated intensively
and succeeded as of 30 November 2016 to sign the agreement
on oil production reduction. Thus, at the end of 2016, forecasts
on global economy development were relatively optimistic, given
that the international macroeconomic conditions improved both
for advanced economies and emerging economies.
1.1
World economy
International Monetary Fund estimates that in 2016 world
economy increased by 3.1 percent, lower than the level of 2015.
This development was determined by the mitigation in advanced
economies increase from 2.1 percent in 2015 to 1.6 percent in
2016 under the circumstances the emerging economies increased
in 2016 by 4.1 percent, similar level as in 2015. Most of advanced
economies recorded a lower growth rate of the gross domestic
products compared to the previous years. Thus, in 2016, the GDP
of the USA grew on average by 1.6 percent, by 1.0 percentage
points less compared to 2015.
Other advanced economies

In 2016, the economy of euro area
increased by 1.7 percent, of which Germany – 1.9 percent, France
– 1.1 percent, Spain – 3.2 percent and Italy – 1.0 percent (Chart
1.1). The economy of China has also recorded a slight slowdown,
which during 2016 increased on average by 6.7 percent (2015 –
6.9 percent). The macroeconomic situation in the region improved
considerably.
After more quarters of decline, the economy of
Russian Federation managed to record in the fourth quarter of
2016 a positive increase of gross domestic product, which
contributed to the calculation of an annual average contraction of
gross domestic product of 0.2 percent. In the regional context, the

the economy of Ukraine increased on average 2.3 percent in
2016, after which decreased by 9.8 percent in 2015. The economy

2015 (Chart 1.1).
In 2016, foreign exchange markets were marked by the monetary
policy of the Federal Reserve System (Fed). As of 15 December
2015, Fed increased the effective rates range of the federal funds
from 0-0.25 to 0.25-0.5 percent. Although a further increased was
expected more rapidly, Fed managed with caution the economic
trends and on 14 December 2016 decided a new increase of
interest rates, the new range was between 0.5-0.75 percent. This
determined the U.S. dollar to reach in December 2016 the highest
level of appreciation since 2002, according to the USDX index1
(Chart 1.2).

The monetary policies antagonism of the Fed and ECB determined
overall the U.S. dollar to appreciate in 2016 by 0.2 percent against
the European single currency. As a result of the referendum on the
United Kingdom member-status within the European Union and
further forecasts of Brexit consequences, Pound sterling (GBP)
depreciated
dramatically
compared
to
other
international
currencies in circulation. Pound sterling depreciated against USD
in 2016 on average by 13.3 percent, the level of December 2016
was by 20.0 percent more depreciated than the previous year. At
the same time, as compared with Canadian dollar (CAD), Swiss
franc (CHF) and Swedish krona (SEK), U.S. dollar appreciated
during 2016 on average by 3.6, 2.3 and 1.5 percent, respectively,
while Japanese yen depreciated by 10.1 percent (Table 1.1).
Most of emerging economies currencies have also depreciated
against U.S. dollar. Thus, in 2016 compared to U.S. dollar, Russian
ruble (RUB) depreciated on average by 10.1 percent, Ukrainian
hryvnia (UAH) by 17.0 percent, Romanian leu (RON) by 1.3
percent, Belarusian ruble (BYR) by 25.3 percent, Kazakhstan
tenge (KZT) by 54.3 percent, Turkish lira (TRY) by 11.1 percent,
Chinese yuan (CNY) by 5.8 percent, Argentinian peso (ARS) by
59.4 percent and Brazilian real (BRL) by 4.3 percent. At the same
time, the increase in raw materials prices by the end of 2016
determined the appreciation of some currencies dependent of the
raw materials prices.
Thus, in December 2016, the value of
Russian ruble and Brazilian real was by 10.7 and 13.6 percent,
respectively, more compared to December 2015 (Table 1.1).
In the second half of 2016, the international prices of raw
materials
reversed
its
downward
trend
upwardly,
which
contributed to the decrease in annual average decline.
Thus,
according to IMF data, during 2016, international raw materials
prices decreased on average by 10.0 percent, of which food
prices increased by 2.0 percent, metal prices decreased by 5.4
percent and energy resources prices decreased by 16.5 percent
(Chart 1.3). The increase in raw materials prices was generated
by the following factors:
the increase of consumption in the
advanced economies due to the incentive monetary policies;

trade imbalances, precarious harvest of some agricultural culture

generated by OPEC decision to decrease the oil production
volume; the optimism of markets related to the ample investment
program announced in the U.S. presidential election campaign.

In January 2016, international oil prices reached the lowest post-
crisis level 2008 due to the global oil surplus and lifting the
oil embargo for Iran. This shock determined the oil producing
countries to work together to solve the global oil surplus issue.
Thus, the ﬁrst negotiations between OPEC and other oil producers
were carried out in April in Doha, which ﬁnely failed. Thereafter, in
September 2016, a new tentative of negotiations in Alger reached
a non-formal compromise to decrease the oil production volume.
During the meeting of 30 November 2016, Vienna, OPEC member
countries decided to decrease the oil production volume up to
32.5 million barrels per day. Subsequently, the Russian Federation
joined the decision to reduce the oil production volume and intends
to reduce the oil production by 300 thousand barrels per day in
the ﬁrst half of 2017, as well other non-OPEC countries intend to
reduce the oil production volume by about 558 thousand barrels
per day during the reporting period. However, both oil demand
and supply exceeded the levels of 2015, annual average surplus
being 0.7 million barrels per day (Chart 1.4).
Statistically, Brent average oil price was USD 44.0 per barrel,
decreasing by 15.9 percent compared to the average of 2015. WTI
average oil price was USD 43.2 per barrel, decreasing by 11.3
percent compared to the average of 2015. Urals average oil price
was USD 41.7 per barrel, decreasing by 18.6 percent compared
to the average of 2015. The decrease in oil prices during the last
two years has also determined the decrease in the import prices
for natural gas. Thus, the average import price for natural gas
amounted to USD 193.5/1000 m3, by about 24.4 percent less than
in 2015 (Chart 1.5).
1.2
Important economies
In 2016, the GDP of the USA grew by 1.6 percent, by 1.0
percentage points less compared to 2015. The drastic slowdown
of the economic growth was based on the adverse consequences
of the long of low energy resources prices over industrial output
and the decrease in net export value under the conditions of the
USD appreciation and low inﬂation level.
In 2016, industrial
production decreased on average by 1.0 percent. The average
unemployment rate was 4.9 percent in 2016, the lowest level of
4.6 percent being recorded in November 2016. Consumer prices
were increasing, annual average inﬂation was 1.3 percent during
the reporting period, after the stagnation of consumer prices in
2015.
GDP in euro area increased by 1.7 percent during the reporting
period. Industrial output contributed signiﬁcantly to the euro area
growth, which increased in 2016 on average by 1.3 percent, of
which by 1.0 percent in Germany, 0.4 percent in France and by
2.0 percent in Italy. At the same time, construction production


Chapter 1. External environment
11
increased by 1.6 percent, exports by 2.4 percent and imports by
1.0 percent. The unemployment rate in euro area decreased up
to 9.6 percent in December 2016, the lowest level as from May
2009. Thus, the annual average unemployment rate in euro area
was 10.0 percent. Annual inﬂation in euro area increased up to
1.1 percent in December 2016, as a result of the consumption
increase and the gradual increase in energy resources prices.
In 2016, the GDP of Romania grew by 4.8 percent, by 0.9
percentage points more compared to 2015. The industrial output
increased by 1.3 percent during the analysed period and
construction production decreased by 4.8 percent, while exports
increased by 5.1 percent and imports by 7.0 percent.
The
unemployment rate remained stable close to the value of 6.0
percent. Consumer prices decreased in Romania on average by
1.6 percent during 2016, in December decreasing by 0.5 percent
compared to prices of December 2015 (Table 1.2).

percent during 2016. It should be mentioned that, according to
the data revised by Rosstat, GDP decreased by 2.8 percent in
2015 compared to 3.7 percent initially estimated. According to
preliminary data, industrial output increased by 1.1 percent in
2016, while construction production decreased by 4.3 percent and
retail trade by 5.2 percent. In 2016, exports decreased by 16.9
percent and imports – by 0.3 percent compared to 2015.
In
December 2016, the unemployment rate decreased up to the
level of 5.3 percent, overall, however, the average unemployment
rate was 5.5 percent in 2016. During 2016, prices recorded a
slowdown trend,
inﬂation decreasing up to 5.4 percent in
December 2016. Overall, consumer prices increased on average
by 7.0 percent compared with the average of 2015 (Table 1.2).
After which the economy of Ukraine recorded a drastic decrease of
9.8 percent in 2015 and in 2016 it increased by 2.3 percent. The
economic recovery was due to the increase in industrial production
by 2.8 percent, increase in construction production by 13.1 percent
and agricultural production by 6.1 percent. At the same time,
exports decreased by 4.6 percent during the reporting year, while
imports increased by 4.6 percent.
Following the slowdown in
consumer prices growth rate in the ﬁrst half of the year, this
accelerated by the end of the year due to the depreciation of
Ukrainian hryvnia.
Thus, in December 2016, consumer prices
increased by 12.2 percent compared to the same month of 2015,
while annual average inﬂation was 13.8 percent during 2016 (Table
1.2).

dynamic was by 4.6 percentage points higher than in 2015. In the
ﬁrst half of 2016, the economy of the Republic of Moldova recorded
a shy annual dynamic of only 1.3 percent, due to the drought
of 2015, the conditions of an unfavorable regional economic
environment and domestic economic and political uncertainty that

dictated a mediocre domestic demand and drop of investments.
The economic boost strengthened its positions in the second half
of the year, GDP recording and increase of 6.3 percent and 6.5
percent in the third and the fourth quarters, respectively, mostly
due to favorable weather conditions that lifted the agricultural
sector’s performance.
In the light of uses (Chart 2.1), the positive evolution of the
economic activity in 2016 was driven, for the most part, by the
increase of exports by 8.8 percent,
contributing with 3.8
percentage points to the GDP dynamic, predominantly following a
positive activity in the agricultural sector in the second half of the
year. At the same time, a signiﬁcant share (3.2 percentage points)
of the economic growth may be attributed to the increase of the
households’ ﬁnal consumption by 3.6 percent compared with
2015.
In the ﬁrst six months of the year, its evolution was
moderate, reﬂecting a shy domestic demand. Later, in the second
half of the year, the consumption dynamic was boosted by the
agricultural sector, outlined by the increase of in kind goods and
services consumer component.
Late 2016 witnessed also the
acceleration
of
goods
purchase
and
services
purchase
components. Final consumption of public administration reduced
by 0.5 percent compared with 2015.
At the same time, the
investment component yielded 1.5 percentage points to the GDP
dynamic, which was due to the positive contribution of stock
variation subcomponent, given that the gross formation of the ﬁx
capital recorded a recession of 3.0 percent compared with 2015.
The economic activity dynamic was signiﬁcantly mitigated by the
growth of imports by 5.9 percent compared with 2015.
By categories of resources (Chart 2.2), the positive evolution of
GDP was predominantly determined by the pronounced increase
of gross value added in agriculture.
Thus, after a negative
contribution of 2015, generated by the drought, in 2016, as a
result of favorable weather conditions, the gross value added in
agriculture increased by 18.2 percent and brought a share of 2.2

Household consumption
In 2016, ﬁnal household consumption increased by 3.6 percent,
recording a level of 5.9 percentage points higher than in 2015.
The dynamic of household consumption was determined by the
increase in all major subcomponents. Thus, the consumption in
kind grew by 9.0 percent, generating a contribution of 1.4
percentage points to the formation of the annual rhythm of the
ﬁnal household consumption, mostly due to the positive trend in
the agricultural sector. Also, in the reference period, the expenses
for goods procurement and expenses for services procurement
increased by 3.0 and 2.7 percent, respectively (Chart 2.3). Their
positive, but shy dynamic can be attributed to an average
evolution of the disposable income of the population.
In 2016, the disposable income of the population saw a positive
dynamic, however considerably lower than the one signaled in
2015.
In this sense, during the reference period, the annual
rate of income increase recorded a level of 5.3 percent or by
5.4 percentage points lower than the level of 2015 (Chart 2.4).
The employed activity, social beneﬁts and remittances, whose
evolution generated an impact of 2.9, 2.4 and 0.6 percentage
points, respectively, contributed the most to annual growth rate
formation of the household disposable income. It should be noted
that the contribution of employed activity and social beneﬁts
dropped by 1.3 and 0.5 percentage points as compared to 2015,
while the contribution of remittances decreased by 1.0 percentage
points.
In 2016, annual growth rate of household disposable
income in real terms recorded a level of -1.0 percent, by 1.9
percentage points lower than in 2015.
Investments
In 2016, the gross formation of capital recorded a positive trend,
exclusively due to the stock variation evolution, as a result of a
good agricultural year, given that the gross formation of the ﬁxed
capital sped its decrease compared with the previous time interval
(from -1.2 percent to -3.0 percent).
Negative evolutions were signaled in investments of long-term
tangible assets as well, the negative rhythms being a more acute
dynamic of up to -13.9 percent. Economic and political uncertainty,
discreet increase of new real estate credits (1.6 percent) and the
decrease in number of construction authorizations for residential
buildings (-8.5 percent) were some of the factors that characterized
the negative evolution of investments in 2016.
All subcomponents, predominantly investment in equipment,
machines and means of transport, as well as investments in

resident buildings recorded a negative annual dynamic, according
to the types of ﬁxed assets, signaling a drop of 19.1 and 20.6
percent, respectively (Chart 2.5).

In terms of funding sources to achieve the investment process, all
subcomponents registered a negative dynamic (Chart 2.6).
The most important sources of investment ﬁnancing – economic
agents’ and households’ own funds (64.9 percent share), had the
most negative contribution, recording a prominent drop of 9.7
percent. This fact can, to a certain extent, also be attributed to
the negative evolution of the real disposable income compared
with the level of 2015. Public sector capital accrual also registered
a downward trend. Thus, in 2016, state budget means designated
for investment process shrank by 49.1 percent compared with
2015.
Agricultural production
The volume of agricultural production increased by 18.6 percent
in 2016 compared with 2015, following a pronounced drop (Chart
2.7). This evolution was determined by the increase in volume of
plant production by 26.0 percent, contributing by 17.6 percentage
points to the boost of global agricultural output.
According to the operative data, the positive dynamic in plant
production was determined by the increased in harvest of “wheat”,
“sun-ﬂower”, “potatoes”, “corn” and “fruits, nuts, and berries” by
39.8, 38.9, 35.0, 28.8 and 19.2 percent, respectively, contributing
overall by 13.9 percentage points. At the same time, the volume
of animal production during the analyzed period increased only by
3.1 percent. The increase in animal production was determined
by the increase in “production of cattle and poultry” (4.8 percent)
and “eggs” (7.0 percent).
Industrial production
The volume of industrial production in all forms of ownership
increased in real terms by 0.9 percent in 2016, as compared with
2015 (Chart 2.8). This evolution was determined mostly by the
increase in volume of “manufacturing industry” production by 1.8
percent.
At the same time, the “extractive industry” and
“production and supply of electricity, gas, hot water and air
conditioning” sectors recorded decreases in industrial production
volume by 15.8 and 1.0 percent, respectively. The manufacturing
recorded the most signiﬁcant increases in the following industrial
activities:
“wood processing,
manufacture of wood and of
products of wood and cork, except furniture; manufacture of
articles
of
straw
and
plaiting
materials”
(50.2
percent),
“manufacture of textile products” (31.8 percent), “manufacture of
furniture” (27.5 percent), “manufacture of paper and products of
paper” (21.7 percent), “manufacture of chemical substances and
products” (17.2 percent) and “manufacture of wearing apparel”
(15.1 percent).
Transport of goods
In 2016,
enterprises of rail,
road,
river,
and air transport
transported goods by 1.6 percent less than in 2015 (Chart 2.9).
This evolution was determined by the decrease in annual terms in
volume of goods transported by air (-15.9 percent), rail (-15.8

goods by road increased in annual terms by 4.7 percent.

activity increased by 1.1 percent, by 7.5 percentage points more
than in 2015. At the same time, the turnover for market services

provided to people in the same period recorded an increase in

Foreign trade
After, the foreign trade2 shrank considerably in 2015, in 2016, a
slight reanimation was attested 3.Thus, the value of exports in U.S.
dollars increased in annual terms by 4.0 percent, while imports
saw only a 0.8 percent boost. It should be noted that the positive
trend of the annual rate in exports was mostly inﬂuenced by a
favorable agricultural year and a high external demand from EU
countries. At the same time, the annual rate of imports in 2016,
although registered a positive pattern, it still keeps a modest trait.
Nevertheless, in the second half of 2016, clear revival signs related
to import had been outlined.
The quarterly development of the annual rate of exports by
categories of countries (Chart 2.11), shows a gradual growth of
demand from the EU countries, while the demand from CIS keeps
holding a negative position. Thus, if in the ﬁrst quarter of 2016
the exports to the EU reduced the annual export rate by 11.0
percentage points,
in late 2016 they generated a positive
contribution of 21.9 percentage points. At the same time, exports
to CIS countries during 2016 generated negative contributions,
while those countries falling in the category of “Rest of the world”
had a mixed evolution.
The development of exports by groups of goods (Chart 2.12),
shows that the annual exports rate pronounced ﬂuctuations are
determined, predominantly, by the evolution of “foodstuff and
animal products, beverages and fats” group. Thus, if the ﬁrst
half of 2016 the dynamic of this group was outlined by a dry year
(2015), the second half of the year effects of a good agricultural
year were marked. At the same time, the growth of the annual
exports rate in 2016 was also due to the growth of exports of
“textiles and clothes” and “vehicles, optical instruments, sound
recorders and reproducers” by 13.3 and 3.5 percent, respectively.
Also, the drop in exports of “mineral products” (-15.7 percent)
and “chemical and wood products” (-15.2 percent) alleviated the
annual exports rate.
During 2016, the annual imports rate recorded an upward trend,
increasing from -29.4 percent in the fourth quarter of 2015 to 9.8
percent in the fourth quarter of 2016. This evolution was driven by
the dynamic of imports from all groups of countries (Chart 2.13).
By groups of goods (Chart 2.14), the growth in value of imported
goods in 2016 was mostly determined by the evolution of imports

By national economy breakdown by activities, the growth of
employed persons was determined by the positive contribution
brought in by the agricultural and trade sectors (Chart 2.15). Thus,
the number of employed persons in these sectors increased by
7.6 percent and 5.3 percent, respectively. A drop in number of
employed persons was recorded in “construction” by 6.8 percent,
“other activities” by 5.8 percent and “social services” by 5.2
percent.
At the same time, the annual growth rate of economically active
people had a shy dynamic in 2016, of only 0.6 percent, decreasing
by 2.1 percentage points compared with 2015 (Chart 2.15).
Also, the number of unemployed people dropped from 62.1
thousand in 2015 to 53.3 thousand in 2016. The unemployment
rate had a downward trend, reducing from 4.9 percent in 2015 to
4.2 percent in 2016, being by 0.7 percentage points lower than in
2015 (Chart 2.16).
An increase in occupation and activity rates was recorded from
40.3 percent in 2015 up to 40.8 percent in 2016 and from 42.4
percent in 2015 up to 42.6 percent in 2016.
According to the data provided by the ANOFM, the number of
people laid off increased signiﬁcantly in 2016 (by 38.0 percent)
compared with the average for January-December 2015, and the
number of vacancies grew by 16.7 percent.

compared with 2015, while deﬂated by CPI grew only by 1.6
percent. At the same time, the average number of employees in
the national economy dropped by 2.0 percent (Chart 2.17).

The dynamic of the average wage in the national economy
increased by 10.3 percent compared with 2015. The budgetary
and the real sectors of economy recorded an increase in average
wage of 5.7 and 12.0 percent, respectively, compared with 2015.
The annual growth rate of the real average wage in economy
accounted for 3.6 percent in 2016. The average wage in real terms
in the real sector increased by 5.2 percent, while the real wage in
the budgetary sector decreased by 0.7 percent (Chart 2.18).

accounted for 1.4 percent, keeping to the level of 2015 (Chart
2.19). The annual growth rate of labour productivity in industry
accounted for 0.7 percent, by 4.2 percentage points lower than in
2015. As a result, the annual growth rate of the labour unit cost
in industry increased by about 4.0 percentage points up to 0.9
percent.

Consumer price index
In 2016, the annual average CPI inﬂation rate accounted for 6.4
percent, by 3.3 percentage points lower than in 2015.
The
downward inﬂation evolution was mostly due to a shy domestic
demand, high level of the base period of 2015, and a high
agricultural harvest contributing to the decrease in foodstuff
prices. At the same time, the downward inﬂation trend was driven
by low pressures from regulated prices due to downward
adjustment in prices for gas and electricity in the ﬁrst six
month

of the year, and due to the gradual dissipation of the effect of

of

for

in

2015.
The
disinﬂationary effect of the aforementioned factors was mostly
mitigated by the national currency evolution against the U.S.
dollar and the upward trend of food and oil prices on the
international market.
During 2016,
the annual inﬂation rate had a pronounced
downward trend (Chart 2.20). It dropped from 13.6 percent in
December 2015 to 2.4 percent in December 2016. In August, it
recovered and returned to the range of 5.0 percent ±1.5
percentage points, stipulated in the Medium-term monetary policy
strategy, accounting for 3.6 percent. Later, the annual inﬂation
rate continued to drop to the lower limit of the aforementioned
range.
In 2016, core inﬂation situated higher than the total inﬂation. Its
annual rate decreased to 4.5 percent in December 2016, being by
10.1 percentage points lower than in late 2015. The annual rate
of foodstuff prices dropped from 14.6 percent in January 2016, to
2.8 percent in late 2016 (Chart 2.21). The annual rate of regulated
goods and services prices, after a pronounced decrease in the ﬁrst
8 months of the year, stopped in the negative range and thus, in
December accounted for -1.1 percent. In 2016, the annual price of
fuel had negative value, except for the ﬁrst and the last months of
the year.
Foodstuff prices (3.0 percentage points) (Chart 2.22) had the
largest contribution to the formation of the annual average inﬂation
rate in 2016. Core inﬂation related prices contributed by about
2.5 percentage points, while regulated services prices – by 1.0
percentage points. Fuel prices generated a negative contribution
of 0.1 percent.
Core inﬂation
During 2016, the annual core inﬂation rate followed a downward
trajectory, dropping from 14.6 percent in December 2015 to 4.5
percent in December 2016.
The downward trend of the core inﬂation was driven by the
negative aggregate demand and by the effect of a high base
period of 2015.
During the reporting period, annual core inﬂation was mainly
fueled by the increase in prices of the subcomponents “clothing”,

“footwear”, “means of transport, auto parts”, “household articles”,
“cigarettes” – the latter being inﬂuenced by the amendments to
the ﬁscal policy on excise increase (Chart 2.23).
The increase in prices of the aforementioned subcomponents was
driven by the increase in costs due to the depreciation of

national currency against major currencies of the trading partners
of the Republic of Moldova, as the core inﬂation is a component
highly dependent on the exchange rate behaviour. Thus, in 2016
compared with 2015, the ofﬁcial average exchange rate of MDL
recorded a depreciation of 5.9 percent against U.S. dollar and of
5.5 percent against the European single currency.
In December 2016, the annual core inﬂation dynamic was mostly
driven by the increase in price for “cigarettes” (15.3 percent),
“footwear” (9.4 percent), “education” (8.7 percent), due to the
increase of tuition for higher education and secondary vocational
education.
Food prices
In 2016, the annual food prices rate recorded a downward trend.
Thus, in December 2016 it reached 2.8 percent, being by 11.5
percentage points lower than in December 2015 (Chart 2.24).
The bumper crop in the agricultural sector and the shy domestic
demand can be considered the main internal factors that led to
the pronounced mitigation of the annual food price rate in 2016. It
should be noted that the pronounced mitigation rate of the annual
food prices rate in 2016 was driven by the high base period in
2015.
Food prices on the global market recorded a mixed evolution. Thus,
if in the ﬁrst half of 2016 the annual food price increase rate on
the global market was situated in the negative range, the second
half of the year it sped up, recording in late reporting year 11.0
percent, or by 28.4 percentage points more than in December
2015.
The mitigated annual food prices rate increase in 2016 was driven
by the drop in contribution of groups “fresh vegetables”, “milling
and bakery products”, “potatoes”, “ﬁsh and ﬁsh products”, “fresh
fruits” and “milk and milk products” (Chart 2.24).
Regulated prices
The annual rate of regulated prices recorded a downward dynamic
in the positive range in the ﬁrst seven months of 2016, dropping
from 13.5 percent in December 2015 to 6.9 percent in July 2016.
As of August, the annual rate of regulated prices positioned in the
negative range and remained there until late 2016, recording in
December the value of -1.1 percent (Chart 2.25).

This development was mostly driven by the gradual removal of
the effect related to the increase in electricity tariff in summer
2015, and also to the downward review of electricity and network
gas tariff in the ﬁrst half of 2016. At the same time, the drop in
annual rate of regulated prices occurred due to decrease in the
positive contribution of medicine prices, following the amendments
to the law on pharmaceutical activity in spring 20164, and the
4According to the new provisions, as at 1 April 2016, pharmacies apply mark-ups

Chapter 2. The economic situation of the Republic of Moldova
21
negotiations in fall 2016 between the Government and pharmacy
networks5 aiming to reduce medicine prices for the ﬁnal consumer.
As a result, in December 2016, medicine prices were by 1.2
percent lower than in the same month of 2015.
In the fourth
quarter of 2016, the regulated prices were inﬂuenced by the
positive contribution of subcomponent central heating, following
the decision of National Energy Regulatory Agency (ANRE) to
increase the tariff on heating supplied to consumers from Chis, in˘au
by 8.2 percent compared with the previous tariff, in effect since
2011. As the new electricity tariff had been adopted, hot water
supply tariff was also adjusted. Nevertheless, the impact on the
regulated prices was a small one. At the same time, in the fourth
quarter of 2016, the prices of telecommunication services recorded
an increase by 4.0 percent, following the amendment of the tariff
grid for international calls within the landline services provided by
S.A. Moldtelecom, as at 1 November 20166. During the reporting
period, negligible contributions to the annual dynamic of prices
for regulated services were generated by prices for healthcare
services and transport services (Chart 2.25).
Fuel prices
In 2016, the annual growth rate of fuel prices was characterized
by a high volatility. Thus, in the ﬁrst eight months of 2016, the
annual rate of fuel prices recorded a downward dynamic, dropping
from 3.2 percent in December 2015 to -5.5 percent in August 2016
(Chart 2.26).
This dynamic was predominantly driven by the decrease in price
for fuel, following the signiﬁcant cut in international prices
for

oil (average price for Urals oil in the ﬁrst eight months of 2016
accounted for USD 39.3 per barrel, by 23.5 percent less than the
average price for oil in 2015). It should also be mentioned that
as of May, ANRE adopted a new methodology on oil products
price formation and application. According to its initial version,
ANRE, every 14 calendar days, on Tuesday, sets the ceiling retail
price7 of petrol, diesel fuel, liqueﬁed gas that cannot be exceeded
by market operators.
Later, the ceiling price for liqueﬁed gas
had been excluded from the aforementioned methodology. These
amendments had an impact on fuel prices in the following months
of the year. As of August, the annual rate of fuel prices had an
upward dynamic, increasing to 1.6 percent in December.
The
annual upward rate of fuel prices was determined, in the second
half of the year, by the mitigation of negative pressures from
fuel and bottled gas prices following an increase in international
oil prices. Thus, the price of Urals oil in August-December 2016
reached an average of USD 46.4 per barrel, increasing by 20.2
percent compared with oil price in the ﬁrst seven months of
the year.
During 2016, contribution of ﬁrewood prices to fuel
prices dynamic was positive and accounted on average for 3.3
percent. Coal prices generated, in 2016, a minor contribution to
the dynamic of fuel prices.

Industrial production prices
The annual growth rate of industrial production prices (IPPI)
recorded in 2016 a downward trend. Thus, in December 2016, it
recorded a level of 2.8 percent or by 6.3 percentage points less
than in December 2015.
This evolution was marked by the
dynamic in prices for products delivered both on the foreign and
domestic markets (Chart 2.27).
The dynamic of the annual rate of prices in industry was mainly
determined by the evolution of prices in manufacturing (Chart
2.28).
Thus, in December 2016, the prices in manufacturing

industry contributed by 2.5 percentage points to the IPPI annual
rate formation, or by 5.3 percentage points less than in 2015. The
pronounced decrease in contribution of manufacturing prices in
2016 was determined by the slowdown of growth rates of prices
in “food industry”, “manufacture of beverages” and “tobacco
products”.
The slowdown of the annual rate of prices in the
aforementioned industries was mostly driven by the decrease
in prices for raw material, due to a good agricultural year. Also, in
December 2016, prices in energy sector increased by 1.8 percent,
generating a contribution of only 0.2 percentage points (Chart
2.28). The growth of prices in energy sector was driven by the
Chis, in˘au municipality “central heating” tariff adjustment. Similar
to previous periods, the contribution of prices related to extraction
industry was negligible.
Construction prices
During 2016, the annual growth rate of construction prices
oscillated around the value of 8.6 percent. In the fourth quarter of
2016, it amounted to 8.9 percent, increasing by 0.7 percentage
points compared with the fourth quarter of 2015 (Chart 2.29).
In the structure of the national economy, in the fourth quarter
of 2016, the following sectors recorded the largest increases
compared with the same period of 2015: “building construction”
(14.0 percent), “other constructions” (13.7 percent), “agriculture”
(9.3 percent) and “electric power” (8.3 percent).
Producer prices for agricultural products
In 2016, producer prices for agricultural products decreased by
3.4 percent compared with 2015. This development was mainly
determined by the decrease in prices of plant products by 4.1
percent.
At the same time, the prices of livestock products
recorded a shy development, decreasing in annual terms by 0.2
percent (Chart 2.30).
The development of plant products prices was mainly determined
by the decrease in prices of “cereal and grain legumes” (-8.7
percent), “sunﬂower” (-3.4 percent), “rape” (-3.1 percent) and
“fruits and berries” (-2.2 percent). At the same time, the decrease
in prices of livestock products was determined by the decrease
in prices of “eggs” (-0.9 percent) and “cattle and poultry” (-0.7
percent).


Chapter 2. The economic situation of the Republic of Moldova

2.3
Public sector
In January-December 2016, the dynamic of revenue collected to
the national public budget recorded an annual increase of up to
5.2 percent, compared with 2.9 percent in 2015, accounting for
MDL 45 946.9 million (Chart 2.31).
Relative to GDP, revenue collection decreased in 2016 by 1.5
percentage points, compared with 2015, accounting for 34.2
percent.

The largest part of budget revenues was accrued from tax and
fees, holding a share of 63.6 percent (MDL 29231.0 million).
Contributions and compulsory insurance premium accounted for
28.9 percent (MDL 13 271.1 million), while the share of other
revenues and grants made up 4.5 percent (MDL 2 071.8 million)
and 3.0 percent (MDL 1 373.0 million) respectively out of the total
budget revenues.
During the reporting year, the Republic of Moldova received
ﬁnancial assistance for its budget as external grants amounting to
MDL 1 373.0 million or by 29.7 percent less than in 2015. The
assistance from international ﬁnancial organizations and donor
countries is a major contribution to the economic recovery and
reform implementation aiming to upgrade and relaunch the
Republic of Moldova.
The national public budget expenditure amounted to MDL 48 434.0
million in the reference year, recording an increase of 4.4 percent
compared to 2015. Of the total expenses, the largest share was
held by social insurance and assistance (35.6 percent), education
(17.7 percent), and health protection (13.4 percent). 10.6 percent
and 9.5 percent were allocated to general state services and
economy-related services, respectively, while national defense,
public order and national security held 8.0 percent of expenditure,
other sectors beneﬁtting from a more modest funding.
Public expenditure to GDP accounted for 36.0 percent, or by 1.9
percentage points less than in 2015.
In 2016, the enforcement of the national public budget resulted
in a deﬁcit of MDL 2 487.1 million, by MDL 246.1 million below
the level of 2015. Relative to GDP, this deﬁcit represented 1.8
percent, by 0.4 percentage points below the level of 2015 (Chart
2.32). Funding sources of the national public deﬁcit consisted of
external sources amounting to MDL 3 492.5 million and internal
sources of MDL 606.8 million.
In late 2016, the state debt of the Republic of Moldova accounted
for MDL 50 785.8 million, by MDL 17 276.6 million or 51.6 percent
more than in late 2015. This growth is largely due to the conversion
of state guarantees to state securities with a due date range of 1
to 25 years, amounting to MDL 13.3 billion in October 2016, and
issued by the Ministry of Finance on behalf of the Government of
the Republic of Moldova to secure emergency credits granted by
the National Bank of Moldova between 2014 and 2015, pursuant
to Law no. 235 of 03 October 2016 on the issuance of state bonds


24
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
for enforcing by the Ministry of Finance of payment obligations
derived from state guarantees no. 807 of 17 November 2014 and
no. 101 of 01 April 2015.
In late 2016, the state debt consisted of external public debt (57.6
percent) and internal public debt (42.4 percent).
As at 31 December 2016, the balance of the external public debt,
expressed in foreign currency, increased by 9.55 percent compared
with 01 January 2016 and accounted for USD 1 464.7 million. The
external debt, as value expressed in MDL, increased during the
reporting year by 11.3 percent, amounting to MDL 29 266.2 million.
In late 2016, the internal public debt recorded a value of MDL
21 519.6 million, 3x higher than in 2015. The modiﬁcation of
the internal public debt was mostly driven by the issuance, in
October 2016, of state securities so that the Ministry of Finance
could enforce payment obligations derived from aforementioned
state guarantees amounting to MDL 13.3 billion and increase the
issuance of state securities on the primary market by about 18.5
percent. Thus, in late 2016, the state public debt consisted of
state securities issued on the primary market (28.4 percent or
MDL 6 115.0 million), converted state securities (9.6 percent or
MDL 2 063.4 million) and state securities issued to enforce state
guarantees (62.0 percent or MDL 13 341.2 million).

In 2016, MDL 1 747.1 million (by 67.5 percent more than in 2015)
were used from the national public budget for public debt service,
of which 83.7 percent were designated for internal public debt and

In late 2016, the public debt as share in GDP accounted for 37.8
percent, by 10.4 percentage points above the level of 2015. This
growth was determined by the increase of internal public debt
relative to GDP by 10.1 percentage points, up to 16.0 percent,
while the share of external public debt increased by 0.3 percentage
points, up to 21.8 percent (Chart 2.33).

The deﬁcit of the current account in 2016 accounted for USD
276.58 million (dropping by 33.3 percent compared with 2015)
(Table A.1). Relative to GDP, it made up 4.1 percent and reduced
by 2.3 percentage points compared with 2015 (Chart 2.34). The
improvement of the current account balance in absolute terms,
recorded in 2016, resulted mostly from the increase by 61.9
percent of the excess recorded for services,
followed by an

increase of 3.2 percent in the excess recorded for secondary
income and a decrease of 1.5 percent of the deﬁcit recorded
in the foreign trade with goods. In 2016, the volume of the foreign
trade with goods (FOB-FOB) amounted to USD 5 151.34 million,
recording a small change compared with 2015 (+1.0 percent). The
deﬁcit of foreign trade with goods accounted for USD 2 056.62
million and decreased by 1.5 percent, because exports increased
more than imports. Ratio between the deﬁcit of foreign trade with
goods and GDP was of 30.5 percent, by 1.6 percentage points
less than in 2015. The coverage of imports of goods with exports
accounted for 42.9 percent, by 1.0 percentage points more than
in 2015.
Value of export of goods (FOB) recorded in the balance of
payments accounted for USD 1 547.36 million (+2.7 percent
compared with 20159).
Even though the physical volume of
exported goods recorded signiﬁcant growth in the third and fourth
quarters of 2016, their value increased insigniﬁcantly compared
with 2015 due to dropping prices. Simultaneously, the prices of
imported goods dropped, however the dropping rate had a
downward trend. It was driven by the decision of OPEC and other
oil exporting countries taken in November 2016 about the cut
crude oil extraction volumes, leading to the increase in prices at
the end of the year. Thus, if in the ﬁrst through third quarters of
2016 the exchange ratio in the foreign trade with goods of the
Republic of Moldova showed a positive dynamic (prices of
exported goods reducing more slowly than those of imported
goods),
in the fourth quarter of 2016 the exchange ratio
worsened.
In regional terms, the evolution of exports of the Republic of
Moldova was driven by the economic situation of the major trade
partners from CIS countries. Even though in the fourth quarter of
2016 the Russian Federation overcame the economic recession,
recording a positive GDP dynamic, the decline in the ﬁrst through
the third quarters marked the exports of Moldova to Russia,
dropping by 2.9 percent compared with 2015 (according to

customs statistics). At the same time, after the recession of 2015,
Ukraine recorded a real growth in 2016, positively affecting the
exports of the Republic of Moldova to this country (+8.4 percent).
The cancellation of temporary taxes to the imported goods
introduced previously to stabilize the balance of payments that
occurred in late 2015 contributed to the increase of exports to
Ukraine. In Belarus, however, the economic recession continued
in 2016 as well, which resulted in a drop of exports of the Republic
of Moldova to Belarus by 21.3 percent.
It is also outlined a
signiﬁcant drop in exports to Kazakhstan (by 4.4 times), resulted
from the ban, as of 01 July 2016, of transit on the territory of the
Russian Federation of the goods that enter the territory of Ukraine
and major depreciation of Kazakhstan tenge.
The rate of exports to the European Union states was uneven,
mirroring the different economic performances of EU member
states. If, per total, exports of goods of the Republic of Moldova
to EU recorded a positive dynamic in the second half of the year,
resulting in a considerable yearly growth, the evolution of exports
to EU member states was different.
Exports to Romania and
Bulgaria increased by 23.2 percent and 2.6 times, respectively,
while the exports to Great Britain and Italy decreased by 18.7
percent and 16.2 percent, respectively. In total, export of goods to
EU increased by 10.4 percent, those to CIS – decreased by 15.8
percent.
The geographic structure of exports of goods (less goods after
processing and other adjustments) in 2016 shows that 56.6 percent
had the European Union countries as destination, 27.5 percent
were delivered to CIS countries, the other 15.9 percent were
exported to other countries (Chart 2.35).

Within the structure of exports by categories, the agri-food
products continued to hold the largest share (60.4 percent), of

19.1 percent compared with 2015), machinery and equipment
(down by 6.2 percent), chemical products (down by 25.7 percent,
predominantly due to the decrease in export of medicine by 35.7
percent), textile and textile articles (up by 27.0 percent due to
three-fold increase of export of knitted and crocheted materials),
glass and glassware (up by 4.8 percent compared with 2015) etc.
were delivered.
Value of imported goods (FOB) recorded in the balance of

compared with 2015).
The geographical structure of imports of goods (less goods for
processing and other adjustments, by principle of country of

European Union countries, 29.5 percent – from CIS countries, and
the other 16.9 percent – from other countries (Chart 2.36).
Within the structure of imports by cathegories of goods, mineral
products traditionally held the largest share of 17.3 percent
(dropping by 14.1 percent compared with 2015),
agri-food
products (up by 3.5 percent, a trend mostly generated by the
growth in import of sugar by 4.5 times), followed by machinery
and equipment (up by 1.9 percent), chemical products (up by 1.3
percent), base metal and metal wear (down by 4.2 percent),

plastic, rubber and articles from such materials (down by 1.2

import of motor vehicles increased by 23.2 percent), textile and

The import of energy products and electricity accounted for USD
604.40 million, decreasing by 14.3 percent compared with 2015;
this trend was generated, overall, by the downward rate of prices

The increase of positive balance of services up to USD 211.88
million resulted from the growth of exports by 8.1 percent, while
imports of services kept a constant trend. The ration between the
balance of services surplus and GDP accounted for 3.1 percent, by
1.1 percentage points higher than in 2015 (Table A.2).
A major increase was recorded in the export of travel services (by
19.9 percent), especially personal travel related to the education
services provided to non-residents (+55.0 percent compared with
2015). Also, the export of transport services (by 9.0 percent),
particularly air transport (+27.7 percent) increased. On the other
hand, export of telecommunication services decreased by -21.6
percent.
Within the structure of external trade with services, the largest
share, both in terms of cash-ins and payments for services, was
traditionally held by transport services – 33.5 percent and 35.7
percent per total, respectively. Travel services accounted for 23.4
percent and 32.2 percent within export and import of services. A
signiﬁcant impact on the balance of services had the processing
services of raw material belonging to others, the level of export
of which recorded 14.1 percent of the total export of services for
2016.
The surplus of primary income went down in 2016 by 1.9 percent
compared with 2015, reaching the level of USD 449.15 million
(Table A.3). Although the compensation of resident employees
received for the work performed for non-resident employers
decreased by 8.8 percent, the outcome of this decrease was
mitigated by the simultaneous decrease by 22.0 percent of
primary cash outﬂows, especially of incomes of the direct foreign
investors (as dividends and reinvested proﬁts).
The ration
between the primary income balance and GDP accounted for 6.7
percent, by 0.3 percentage points less than in 2015.
The positive balance of secondary income increased up to USD
1 119.01 million, especially as a result of the growth by 41.7
percent of technical assistance and grants received by the public
administration within international cooperation (Table A.4). At the
same time, the volume of technical assistance and grants received
by other sectors decreased by 10.5 percent compared with 2015.
Personal transfers received from abroad by the residents of the
Republic of Moldova did not change signiﬁcantly (-0.9 percent
compared with 2015). The ration between the secondary income
balance and GDP kept its 2015 value, accounting for 16.6 percent.
Personal remittances received by the residents of the Republic of
Moldova (a new indicator calculated additionally to the balance of
payments according to the methodology BPM6), reﬂecting work
net compensation value (less expenses for travels abroad and


28
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
some fees paid by the residents abroad), personal and capital
transfers among households accounted for USD 1 348.71 million.
The ration between personal remittances and GDP decreased from
21.7 percent in 2015 to 19.9 percent in 2016, due both to the
decrease in value of remittances by 4.7 percent (especially, as a
result of decrease of cash inﬂows from the Russian Federation),
and the increase of GDP.
Capital account recorded a negative balance of USD 46.71 million
(a positive balance of USD 22.88 million was recorded in 2015), as
a result of the decrease of international assistance inﬂow received
by the public administration aimed to fund investment projects
(5.5 times).
As a result of current and capital transactions carried out by the
residents of the Republic of Moldova with abroad during 2016, net
borrowing/lending (amount between the current account balance
and capital account balance from balance of payments) decreased
by 17.5 percent compared with 2015 and made up USD 323.29
million, accounting for 4.8 percent in GDP (6.0 percent in 2015).
In 2016, the ﬁnancial account10 recorded net capital inﬂows in the
amount of USD 289.99 million, due to the net increase of national
economy’s liabilities to abroad by USD 442.63 million, as a result of
actual transactions, while the external ﬁnancial assets of residents
recorded a net increase by USD 152.64 million (Table A.4, chart
2.38).

33
Chapter 3
The activitity of the National Bank of
Moldova
3.1
Achievements of the monetary and
foreign
exchange
policy
during
2016
According to Law on the National Bank of Moldova no.548–XIII of 21
July 1995, the NBM is the central bank of the Republic of Moldova,
being a self-governed public legal entity that established and
promotes the monetary and foreign exchange policy. During 2016,
the NBM acted in accordance with the Medium-term monetary
policy strategy, approved by the Council of Administration of the
National Bank of Moldova on 27 December 2012.
According to this strategy, in order to ensure and maintain price
stability, the NBM has set its inﬂation target, measured by the
consumer price index published monthly by the National Bureau
of Statistics (NBS) of 5.0 percent with a possible deviation of ± 1.5
percentage points.
Quantifying the medium-term macroeconomic situation, trends
and
projections
of
macroeconomic
indicators,
short-
and
medium-term inﬂation outlook, with possible uncertainties and
challenges during the reference period, as well as to mitigate the
disinﬂationary pressures, the National Bank of Moldova has
promoted during 2016 an incentive monetary policy. At the same
time, the efﬁciency of monetary policy stance was affected by the
complexity of risks balance, both internal and external, with a
gradual emphasis of disinﬂationary risks.
The external risks
related to disinﬂationary pressures propagated through the
channel of weak economic activity of the countries in the euro
zone and the continued recession in the Russian Federation – the
main external trading partners of the Republic of Moldova, which
was reﬂected in the further inﬂation dynamic.
The National Bank of Moldova had to adopt gradual monetary
policy calibration measures to ensure real monetary conditions
adequate for lending and savings and to push the domestic
demand, at the same time adjusting the domestic economic
environment
to
the
external
environment’s
volatility
and
uncertainties. In these circumstances, the Executive Board of the
NBM decided to reduce gradual the monetary policy interest rate
during 2016 by 10.5 percentage points, from 19.5 to 9.0 percent
annually.
These
decisions
were
aimed
to
mitigate
disinﬂationary
expectations by strengthening the likelihood of bringing and
maintaining the inﬂation rate within the variation range of ± 1.5
percentage points from the target of 5.0 percent.


34
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
Implementation and promotion of monetary and foreign
exchange policy
The premises to reverse the inﬂation trend that occurred in early
2016 assumed the initiation of a monetary policy relaxation
process. Thus, in late February, the ﬁrst descending adjustment of
the base rate was carried out, the NBM carefully ensuring its
descending rate, its restriction to 0.5 percentage points being
argued by the maintenance of the inﬂation rate above the upper
limit of the variation rate of ± 1.5 percentage points from the
target of 5.0 percent.
In the immediate period, the NBM operated a more consistent key
rate adjustment, the decision being argued mostly by the outline
of some more accentuated disinﬂationary trends. As a result, the
descending trend of the monetary policy interest rate extended
over March-July period, four consecutive decreases by 2.0 and 3.0
percentage points (up to the level of 10.0 percent) being operate.
The central bank ceased temporarily the downward trend of the
base rate, the latter being kept at the level of 10.0 percent in the
following two monetary policy meetings.
In September-October, the NBM continued to carefully tighten the
base rate adjustment step (decreased by 0.5 percentage points
in two consecutive steps); such a conduct was argued by the
downward trend of the annual inﬂation rate and by some moderate
signs related to the ongoing revival of the economic activity in the
third quarter.
The NBM decided to interrupt the descending trend of the monetary
policy interest rate in the last quarter, keeping it to the level of 9.0
percent until the end of the year, due to signiﬁcant uncertainties
and pro-inﬂationary risks.
Thus, during this period, the external inﬂationary risks were
associated with the increase in international prices of food
products, oil and natural gas and the ﬂuctuations on the ﬁnancial
markets. The potential risks and uncertainties of domestic nature
that triggered the inﬂation in the last months of the year came
from the surplus of liquidity in the banking sector, the impact of
amendments to ﬁscal policy-related excise for 2017. Thus, having
analyzed the risk balance, it was noticed the accentuation of
inﬂationary risks on the supply side - toward the middle of 2017.
In this context, the monetary policy measures taken by the NBM
in early 2016 were to spread through different transmission
channels in the national economy, including through the inﬂuence
on the interest rate on credits and deposits in national currency
thus having effects on the inﬂation development.

Also, the NBM kept the symmetry of the interest rate corridor at
the standing facilities around the monetary policy interest rate and
its amplitude to the value of ± 3.0 percentage points. For the year
as a whole, the interest rates on the deposit facility and lending
facility declined from 16.5 percent to 6.0 percent and 22.5 percent
to 12.0 percent, respectively (Chart 3.1).
The slight upward trend of the monthly 91-day state securities
(SS) interest rate in January 2016, was followed by a slow


Chapter 3. The activitity of the National Bank of Moldova
35
downward trend in early February, justiﬁed by the gradual
ampliﬁcation of investment offer for this range of placements; this
trend accentuated further under the inﬂuence of amendments
made to the NBM base rate. In the second half of December, this
rate dropped below the lower limit of the rate corridor set by the
NBM and recorded a minimum value of 5.84 percent.
The special interbank market situation (reduced number of
participants and insigniﬁcant volumes of transactions carried out,
and even the lack of activity on this segment during some
periods) decreased the role of interbank credit/deposit interest
rate in the monetary transmission mechanism.
The average monthly interbank interest rate on credits/deposits
varied from 20.5 to 9.0 percent annually during 2016.
Monetary policy instruments
Monetary market operations, standing facilities and minimum
required reserves mechanism continued to play a leading role
in the monetary policy implementation. The monetary market
operations were carried out at the NBM initiative, the assigned
interest rate being that of monetary policy. The standing facilities
were used at the banks’ own discretion. The required reserves
mechanism kept its austerity during the entire year by keeping the
minimum required reserves rates applicable to liabilities in MDL
and those in foreign currency of the banks at the level of 2015.
Monetary market operations
The surplus of liquidity in the banking system recorded in late
2015 strengthened during 2016, including as a result of foreign
exchange market interventions (foreign currency purchases) in net
volume of USD 397.7 million (about MDL 7.9 billion), and required
a bigger effort to sterilize the central bank, the volume of such
operations increasing compared with 2015. At the same time,

the ﬁrst half of the year was marked by frequent liquidity deﬁcits
which had been covered through repo operations. The volume of
injected liquidities was however, relatively reduced, keeping way
below the level of drains (Chart 3.2).
Issuance of NBM Certiﬁcates
The NBM conducted National Bank Certiﬁcates (NBC) placement
auctions twice a week, with the maturity of 14 days, announcing
the maximum interest rate equivalent to the NBM base rate. Thus,
the NBM absorbed fully the amounts offered by participants.
The average weighted rate of issued NBC accounted for 11.55
percent annually, compared with 15.45 percent recorded in 2015.
Amid the strong increase of net liquidity of banks, the volume of
operations
designated
to
absorb
the
liquidity
recorded
a
signiﬁcant increase, over four times compared to the level of
2015, this situation being also reﬂected in the annual average of
NBC placement stock, which increased from MDL 783.4 million
recorded in 2015, to MDL 2 959.0 million recorded in 2016. The
daily balance of NBC placements gradually increased during the


36
Annual Report (NBM, 2016)
year and reached the maximum of MDL 5 905.6 million in late
2016.
Repo operations
Repo operations were conducted via auctions at a ﬁxed rate (base
rate plus a margin of 0.25 percentage points) twice per week, with
the maturity of 14 days and full allocation.
Bank’s liquidity demand was fully satisﬁed, however, in the ﬁrst
half of the year, it tightened substantially compared with 2015,
followed by the almost systemic lack of offers within repo auctions
until the end of the year. Thus, the daily balance of repo operations
marked the maximum of MDL 615.0 million in April, and in late of
the reference year was null.
Lending activity
In late 2016, the credit portfolio of the National Bank was fully
represented by loans granted to banks in 1992-2002 for crediting
the construction of housing cooperatives (CHC), the balance of
which as at 31 December 2016 accounted for MDL 0.4 million.
Unlike late 2015, both the structure of credits granted to banks
and the balance value saw signiﬁcant changes, due to the events
as follow:
• partial repayment of emergency loans overdue by the banks
undergoing windup (Banca de Economii S.A., BC „BANCA
SOCIAL˘A” S.A. and B.C. ”UNIBANK” S.A.) in the amount of MDL
348.0 million;
• enforcement by the Ministry of Finance (pursuant to Law
no.235 of 03 October 2016) of payment bonds derived from
state guarantees no.807 of 17 November 2014 and no.101 of
01 April 2015 and the issuance, on 04 October 2016, of state
bonds in the amount of MDL 13 341.2 million, equivalent to
the
balance
of
outstanding
emergency
loans
of
the
aforementioned banks undergoing windup and coverage of
the outstanding credit indebtedness of these banks to the
National Bank;
• repayment of loans granted to banks for CHC construction in
amount of MDL 0.3 million.
Standing facilities
Standing facilities regime (overnight deposits and credits) set
by the NBM in 2016, entitled the banks to efﬁciently manage
their liquidity and to grant more ﬂexibility to the NBM related to
monetary policy achievement.
During 2016, simultaneously with the base rate, the interest rates
on standing facilities decreased 7 times, this level falling between
3.0 and 0.5 percentage points range.
Thus, in 2016, the interest rates on overnight deposits evolved
from 16.5 percent annually at the beginning of the year up to 6.0
percent annually at the end of the year while the overnight credit


Chapter 3. The activitity of the National Bank of Moldova
37
interest rate evolved during the same period from 22.5 percent
annually to 12.0 percent annually.
Overnight deposit facility
In 2016, all banks placed overnight deposits with the National Bank
of Moldova.
The total volume of overnight deposits placed by banks at the
National Bank summed up in the reference year MDL 166 194.0
million, which means a daily average balance of MDL 637.9 million
(by MDL 75.8 million or 13.5 percent higher than in 2015).
The increase in volume of overnight placements compared with
2015 occurred due to the increase of liquidities in the banking
system and to more effective management of excess liquidity by
banks, ensured by the appropriate level of interest rates to this
instrument.
The daily average balance of overnight placements of banks
calculated for each month of 2016 recorded uneven values,
between the minimum of MDL 320.2 million in February and the
maximum of MDL 990.2 million in December (Chart 3.3), the
balance value predominantly reﬂecting the evolution of the
excessive liquidity level in the banking system.
During the maintenance period of required reserves in MDL, banks

resorted to deposit facilities with a similar frequency, constituting
minimum amounts at the beginning of the period, overnight
placements increased as the excess liquidity gained by banks
ensured the obligation to comply with the set level of required
reserves, reaching the maximum levels at the end of the period.
Overnight credit facility
Unlike 2015, banks resorted insigniﬁcantly to overnight credit
facility in 2016, only in January, May and June. The amount of
overnight credits granted by the NBM accounted for MDL 141.4
million, recording an average daily balance of MDL 1.9 million,
lower by 7 times than that of 2015.
Overnight credit facility was solicited mainly at the end of the
maintenance periods of the required reserves by a restrained
number of banks, which failed to comply with the requirements of
the required reserves regime.
Required reserves
The required reserves mechanism had an important role in
monetary control and banks’ prudent liquidity management.
Over the whole year, the required reserves regime kept its features
that identiﬁed it during 2015. The decision to uphold the required
reserves in MDL at the level of 35.0 percent of the base was
justiﬁed by the persistence of a large excess liquidity in the banking
system.
Required reserves in foreign currency (USD and EUR) remained
unchanged, maintaining it level of 14.0 percent of the base.
